# OxSteed enterprise operations audit

## Decision and purpose

**Target: 3,500 employees worldwide and 6,000,000 registered users. Decision: the current codebase is a substantial product foundation, but enterprise-scale operational readiness is NOT established.** This report replaces the earlier short audit rather than extending its stale conclusions. It reviews all 65 current sidebar tabs, their supporting workflows, shared infrastructure, and management capabilities needed to operate the company. Each tab starts on a separate PDF page; longer reviews continue onto subsequent pages. Five additional management-workspace reviews address capabilities missing or fragmented across existing pages.

The requirement is not simply that buttons render. OxSteed must prevent unauthorized decisions, duplicate money movement, unowned customer problems, incorrect regional launches, and outages caused by growth. Employees need a controlled way to do their work without becoming platform-wide super administrators. Executives need reliable business evidence, finance needs reconcilable books, and operators need recovery paths when providers, infrastructure, or people fail.

No audit can honestly guarantee that software will never need another fix. The durable objective is **no known critical gaps before expansion, explicit handling of foreseeable failure scenarios, and continuous detection of new risks**. Applying only design recommendations, without the acceptance tests and operational gates in this report, does not meet that objective. This document is an engineering and operating-model assessment, not a legal opinion or certification of financial licensing in any country.

## Current reality and evidence boundary

Source snapshot: `oxsteed/old-file`, branch `old-files-claude`, revision `e7ac9415936c5450dab071cf0ebf9f1c318ea2d8`, reviewed on October 2, 2026. All line references are to that snapshot and must be rechecked after changes. Read-only live verification at 08:55:06 UTC / 04:55:06 EDT found `/ready` healthy, expected/applied migrations **640/640**, with zero pending or unexpected filenames. Database, Redis, realtime initialization, worker initialization, traffic acceptance, and the enabled credit rail were reported healthy. Release was `content-1dd496a985552c72`; Git SHA, image digest, and deployment ID in the readiness payload were **null**. This snapshot is not a guarantee of later uptime or provider readiness.

The former database architecture/startup incident is not the current state: production inspection found the ARM64-compatible `oxsteed/postgis:16-arm64` database and healthy application, Redis, and ClamAV containers. Do not treat the older `exec format error` as an unresolved outage. Conversely, fixing it does not remove the physical-host failure domain.

[GitHub CI run 36984369426](https://github.com/oxsteed/old-file/actions/runs/36984369426) succeeded for revision `e7ac9415`. Its server/client verification and fresh PostGIS migration rehearsal passed. The rehearsal applies all migrations twice to a new database. It does NOT demonstrate a populated, production-sized upgrade, mixed-version compatibility, disaster recovery, or six-million-user performance. The authenticated browser step explicitly printed **0 passed, 2 skipped**, because its base URL and account configuration were absent. Green CI is therefore not authenticated operational certification.

The earlier verification round reported 310 server suites passed, 3 skipped; 3,339 tests passed, 46 skipped. Client localization, type checking, and production build passed in that round. Translation scanning still warned about 322 references without catalog keys (46 raw-key risks and 276 fallback references) and 703 long strings identical to English. Warnings are not the same as broken interpolation gates, but they cannot be described as complete human-verified localization. These counts are snapshot evidence, not a new rerun of every suite in this report-writing session.

The source inventory has **34 regular-admin and 31 super-admin sidebar entries: 65, not 64**. There are additional detail and sub-workspace routes. Existing Customer 360, Business 360, delivery dispatch, delivery tracking, incidents, support operations, credit reconciliation, and reconciliation-exception routes must be extended and made discoverable, not replaced by duplicate systems. See `client/src/admin/adminRouteManifest.js:18-151`, `client/src/admin/AdminApp.jsx:230`, `client/src/admin/AdminApp.jsx:252`, `client/src/pages/DeliveryDispatchPage.jsx`, and `client/src/pages/DeliveryTrackingOpsPage.jsx`.

### Reading findings correctly

- **Confirmed defect:** a specific code behavior contradicts the intended contract, with evidence and a reproducible acceptance test. A static defect does not imply observed production exploitation.
- **Code observation / scale risk:** the implementation is visible, but its harmful outcome under expansion is not yet measured. Validate before choosing a remedy.
- **Verification gap:** necessary evidence is missing; do not convert absence of proof into a passing status or an invented defect.
- **Design requirement:** a capability needed for the target operating model, possibly covered partially elsewhere. Check existing implementations before adding another service.

P0 means an immediate security, integrity, or service-continuity blocker. P1 means a release/expansion blocker for the affected business operation. P2 means an important control or usability deficiency that needs a committed owner and milestone. P3 means lower-risk polish. In tab reviews using qualitative ratings, High corresponds to a material P1 expansion/control risk and Medium to P2 unless a specific priority is stated. A P1 scale gate does not mean the small current deployment is necessarily failing today. Findings use `E` identifiers for shared enterprise controls, `TNN-FNN` for tab-specific issues, and `M` identifiers for management workspaces. Fix shared foundations once and apply them consistently.

## Concrete defects that should be fixed before expansion

The tab reviews are not only a list of hypothetical enterprise features. Source inspection identifies present contract, lifecycle, and authorization defects. The following are priority entry points; the complete numbered reviews contain further findings, evidence, owners, and acceptance criteria. They were not exercised against production money or customer records in this audit.

- **T22-F01 - Orders shipping/cancellation:** `server/controllers/adminOrdersController.js:229,339` calls `withTransaction(callback)`, but `server/utils/transaction.js:39-40` requires `withTransaction(pool, operation)` and calls `pool.connect()`. These mutation paths cannot use that helper correctly. Order operations/payment engineering must fix the call contract and test success, rollback, retries, and safe financial handoff with an actual isolated database.
- **T12-F02 - Quote currency corruption on unrelated edits:** `server/controllers/quoteRequestsController.js:631,816` defaults omitted currency to USD and writes it while a notes/status-only client edit omits currency. Preserve omitted fields and validate explicit monetary changes. Test a non-USD quote through notes/status changes, including approval/history, without altering its currency or price.
- **T20-F01/F02 - Planned work can publish after administrative cancellation:** cancellation does not establish the durable pause expected by scheduling; the scheduler selects candidates before its per-row transaction and rereads only ID/published linkage, not current pause/status (`server/jobs/plannedNeedsScheduler.js:32-79`). Domain scheduling must revalidate eligibility under lock and test cancellation between selection and publication.
- **T10-F01 - Support ticket ownership race:** claim logic reads/checks and then updates by ID, allowing competing operators to overwrite ownership. Support engineering must use an atomic owner/version transition and return a conflict to the losing claimant, preserving the existing stronger live-conversation ownership patterns.
- **T11-F01 / T12-F01 / T23-F01 / T25-F01 - Inconsistent privileged API gates:** live support, quote detail, template grants, and consulting financial actions have role-only or different-grant paths compared with neighboring admin endpoints. Authentication exists; these are least-privilege inconsistencies, not claims of public anonymous access. Security/IAM and each domain must enforce the same scope/action authority through direct APIs, not just menus.
- **T17-F01 - Federated search crosses domain permission boundaries:** the search engine returns requested/default message and payment data under search-log permission without the equivalent message/finance/participant restrictions (`server/controllers/adminSearchController.js:27-30,105-118,153-179`). Scope permitted entity types and fields before querying; a search permission must not become alternate access to private messages or financial records.
- **T18-F01 - Business deletion UI/API mismatch:** `client/src/admin/pages/BusinessModeration.jsx:274-281` sends `{reason}`, but `server/controllers/businessModerationController.js:430-436` requires `confirmed`. Align explicit confirmation and test the real client-to-controller contract without removing authorization or lifecycle safeguards.
- **T35-F01 - Super Dashboard completion rate:** `server/controllers/superAdminController.js:235-239,356-358` combines period completions with lifetime cancellations. Data/operations owners must define and calculate one consistent cohort; old cancellations must not alter a seven-day result merely because the platform grew.
- **T36-F01 / T37-F01 - Currency-ambiguous management totals:** Money and Restaurant Credit overview boundaries aggregate native values without an adequate per-currency reporting contract. This is a reporting problem, not proof that isolated wallets can spend across restaurants. Finance must prevent unqualified mixed-currency sums and approve any reporting FX snapshot.
- **T05-F01 / T10-F02 / T38-F02 - Incomplete operational queues:** paginated server results are not fully reachable from some report/support/compliance screens. An empty filtered page or the first 25/50 results does not establish a cleared workload. Domain owners must expose complete scoped navigation, counts, age, and assignment, verified with records beyond the first page.
- **T56-F01/F04 - Legal assurance is not durable publication:** legal count-query parameter slicing is inconsistent with the bound filters (`server/controllers/legalDocumentsController.js:261-266`), while document drafts/approval/live labels are held in a process-local Map (`server/routes/legalDocuments.js:13,148,196,208,222`). Persist reviewed document versions and connect publication to the real public content and acceptance version; a restart or second node must not erase or disagree on an approved document.
- **T58-F01 - Profile-visibility review lock is not held across the decision:** `server/services/profileVisibilityApprovals.js:257-284` issues a pooled `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` and later an unconditional update without one explicit transaction. Independent reviewers can race. Use one transactional/versioned decision and execute authorized effects idempotently; retain the existing maker/checker restriction.
- **T59-F01 - One Truth pagination does not bound work:** `server/controllers/oneTruthController.js:693-711` loads/enriches all scoped users and builds suspect pairs before slicing the page. At six million users this synchronous request requires redesign as a bounded indexed/queued investigation with safe progress, not a larger page limit. Confirm algorithm cost and memory against realistic distributions.

The highest-value next deliverable is an implementation backlog keyed to these findings, with failing isolated reproductions before fixes, rather than another cosmetic dashboard refresh. High-severity findings must not be closed solely by adding a frontend confirmation dialog, increasing a query limit, or assigning more super-admin accounts.

## Capacity assumptions: users are not concurrent traffic

Six million registered users is a data-size requirement, not a request-rate specification. Use the following **planning assumptions**, not claimed measurements: 20% daily active users means 1.2 million daily users; 60 dynamic requests each means 72 million requests/day, roughly 833 requests/second averaged over 24 hours. A ten-times peak is approximately 8,333 requests/second. A first design test target is 10,000 dynamic requests/second at the representative mix, plus separate media/CDN traffic. Analytics, retries, webhooks, background jobs, and internal API calls must be measured and added rather than hidden in that figure.

Assume 10% of daily users can hold concurrent sockets: 120,000 connections; exercise 240,000 during reconnect/failover stress. This is a test hypothesis, not a fact about usage. Assume 1,500 simultaneously active employees out of 3,500 and test at least 500 admin requests/second during operational incidents. Admin analytical workloads must not steal customer booking or payment capacity. For transactional scenarios, create realistic distributions: active/inactive users, large businesses, hot restaurants, shared drivers, popular helpers, skewed geographic demand, long support histories, and high audit volumes. Six million trivial rows with no relationships is not a meaningful scale fixture.

Proposed initial service objectives, to be approved with budget and product promises: 99.95% monthly availability for critical transaction APIs; database-backed ordinary list/read p95 at or below 500 ms; admin interactive p95 at or below one second for bounded queries; internal committed-event propagation p95 under five seconds; privileged account revocation across HTTP and sockets within 60 seconds; critical financial loss recovery point under five minutes and service recovery under 60 minutes. Financial correctness requires zero duplicate captures/payouts and no unaccounted ledger imbalance; latency averages cannot waive that invariant. External provider response times must be reported separately. Different tiers, countries, and safety-critical operations can need stricter objectives.

Reassess these numbers from telemetry before buying infrastructure. The report does not recommend moving everything to a microservice architecture or immediately running six million users through every endpoint. First isolate workloads, measure bottlenecks, and preserve authoritative domain transactions.

## Shared enterprise findings and controls

### E01 - P1 expansion blocker: one physical production failure domain

**Code observation and live infrastructure evidence.** `README.md:109-122` documents the production MacBook/Colima model; `docker-compose.coolify.yml:2-16,114-118` defines the app with local persistent paths and no high-availability deployment topology. Live inspection places application dependencies on that host. A power loss, disk failure, sleep policy, host upgrade, network loss, or Colima failure can affect the whole business. R2 object storage does not make the compute/database tier highly available.

SRE must establish a supported production topology with at least independent compute failure domains, supervised database failover, shared durable storage, documented network dependencies, and an independent recovery environment. A transition can keep the Mac as a development/recovery node; it should not remain the sole worldwide production dependency at the target size. Use tested infrastructure-as-code and architecture-compatible, immutable images. Acceptance: remove a compute host under representative load without losing committed transactions; restore routing and queues within the approved recovery objective; demonstrate that the operator console and incident channel remain reachable independently of the failed application.

### E02 - P1 expansion blocker: realtime state is process-local

**Code observation.** `server/bootstrap/realtime.js:29-40` initializes Socket.IO without a shared adapter in that bootstrap. `server/services/websocketService.js:46-49` keeps customer connections, agent connections, ticket sockets, and viewers in process-local Maps. Simply adding HTTP replicas can split chat presence, support routing, and fanout across workers.

Adopt a supported cross-node broadcast adapter and an explicit shared presence/assignment contract, including connection ownership, expiration, authorization refresh, and reconnect state reload. An adapter alone does not distribute arbitrary application Maps. If HTTP long-polling stays enabled, configure and test affinity; [Socket.IO's multi-node guidance](https://socket.io/docs/v4/using-multiple-nodes/) explains the adapter and affinity requirements. Acceptance: users attached to different nodes exchange updates; node termination and mass reconnect do not lose durable messages, duplicate case assignments, or expose a prior participant's room. Broadcast only authorized projections, never globally cached sensitive payloads.

### E03 - P1 expansion blocker: worker ownership and lease-loss behavior

**Code observation / verification gap.** `server/bootstrap/backgroundWorkers.js:71-197` starts many scheduled and delivery workers with each app instance. Existing job locks are valuable, not absent: `server/jobs/jobLockingService.js:17-25,55-100` acquires/refreshes leases. However, its wrapper logs renewal errors while the handler continues (`85-86`). A lease can expire and another worker can start unless the domain action has its own idempotency/fencing safeguards.

Separate web and worker capacity, inventory each consumer, declare concurrency and lease rules, and require durable event IDs and transaction-level idempotency for irreversible effects. Long jobs must detect lease loss and fence unsafe writes, or use a queue protocol that safely owns work until completion. Do not stop all work blindly if an already-committed operation needs reconciliation. Acceptance: pause a worker beyond its lease, restart it after a competitor finishes, replay messages in duplicate/reverse order, and prove one financial/stock/status effect with a retained reconciliation trail. Monitor oldest actionable queue age and consumer heartbeat, not process uptime alone.

### E04 - P1 expansion blocker: database budget and admin query isolation

**Code observation / verification gap.** `server/db.js:17-23` defaults the connection pool to ten, caps configured size at 100, and has connection/idle timeouts; this pool declaration does not set a universal statement timeout. `docker-compose.coolify.yml:47` defaults `DB_POOL_MAX` to ten. Multiplying replicas and workers multiplies connections. Raising every pool to 100 is not a capacity plan.

Define database connection budgets per workload, query/lock deadlines, cancellation, bounded pagination, indexes validated by EXPLAIN on representative skew, retention/partition strategy for high-volume events, and asynchronous exports. Use replicas/analytical pipelines for reports with visible lag, while approvals and finance read authoritative state. Consider a pooler only after testing transaction semantics, session features, and migration behavior. Acceptance: six-million-user fixtures, large audit/history tables, and simultaneous admin exports cannot starve quote acceptance, bookings, support, or payment verification; saturation returns explicit retryable states rather than misleading zeros.

### E05 - P1 expansion blocker: global permission model is not a worldwide workforce model

**Code observation / design requirement.** `server/middleware/adminAuth.js:126-177` grants super-admin bypass and intentionally rejects region-specific grants for existing platform-wide delegated endpoints. That fail-closed behavior should stay until actual scoped handlers are implemented. The comment explicitly says current delegated endpoints are platform-wide. It is not enough to let a regional grant pass and then execute an unrestricted query.

Implement employee role bundles, legal-entity/region/team/object scopes, action limits, restricted reads, and separation of duties. Scope every list, detail, export, websocket subscription, approval, and bulk job on the server. Retain narrowly controlled break-glass access with reason, expiry, and independent review. `client/src/admin/lib/GrantGuard.jsx:39,85-88,109-126` uses a five-minute cache and role bypass; navigation caching is a convenience, never the authority. Acceptance: a regional operator cannot access another region by altering IDs, filters, export parameters, or socket rooms; revocation reaches existing sessions; a maker cannot approve their own high-risk change, including via a second browser.

### E06 - P1 verification blocker: green browser step means skipped coverage

**Confirmed verification gap.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml:52-60` supplies optional environment configuration to the browser script. `client/scripts/adminAuthenticated.browser-check.cjs:15-30,90,112,141-142` skips when unconfigured and excludes dynamic record paths from the static route sweep. Actual run 36984369426 confirms the skip. Its smoke assertions are not an exhaustive role/action suite.

Create an isolated, production-like staging environment with deterministic seeded staff/customer/business/driver accounts, provider test fixtures, mandatory role matrix, and resettable domain data. Required release coverage must fail closed when credentials, seeds, or browser infrastructure are missing. Test success, denial, conflict, stale-state, and outage paths. Do not install permanent production test identities that can move real money. Acceptance: report exact tests executed and skipped for all 65 tabs plus supporting detail routes, Chromium/Firefox/WebKit, both themes, mobile, RTL, five languages, and critical mutating journeys.

### E07 - P1 release-control gap: build/deploy identity and promotion evidence

**Code observation and live evidence.** `Dockerfile:34-36` runs the ordinary client build, while CI separately runs `build:ci`. Native Coolify webhook deployment can be independent of CI promotion. Healthy readiness currently reports null git/image/deployment metadata. This does not prove every failed commit is deployed, but it means the audited safety gate and deployed artifact identity are not demonstrated end to end.

Promote an immutable artifact only after mandatory checks pass; bind revision, image digest, schema contract, feature configuration, and deployment ID into a signed/reviewable release record. Add canary traffic, rollback health criteria, migration compatibility, and change approval for financial/configuration changes. Readiness should expose safe identifiers without secrets. Acceptance: a deliberately failing commit cannot become production; operators can map a customer error to one exact artifact/configuration version; rollback restores the prior supported app without reversing irreversible business transactions.

### E08 - P1 integrity/recovery gate: backups must restore a complete business

**Code observation / verification gap.** `scripts/backup-coolify-production.sh:15-23,85-104` creates host-bound database/configuration artifacts and optional mirroring. Its manifest does not itself establish complete upload coverage, independently controlled immutable storage, or recoverability after loss of the host. `scripts/verify-production-restore.sh:40-55` rehearses a temporary database inside the existing production database container: useful, but not an independent disaster restore and potentially competing for resources.

Inventory database, object media, agreements, proofs, provider mappings, jobs/outbox, configurations, and required recovery credentials. Prove encrypted off-host copies, access separation, retention, deletion protection where supported, and independently recoverable access. Treat secrets in configuration backups as highly privileged material without exposing them in reports. Logical dumps remain useful; low-loss recovery needs a tested design such as physical base backups plus archived WAL, which is distinct from `pg_dump` according to [PostgreSQL 16 PITR documentation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/16/continuous-archiving.html). Acceptance: recover on a fresh host with production network unavailable, verify balances/relationships/media, reconcile provider outcomes before resuming workers, and record actual recovery point/time.

### E09 - P1 observability gap: readiness is not continuous business health

**Code observation.** `server/services/readinessService.js:45-58` compares migration filenames; this predicate alone does not verify execution status/checksums. Startup migration checks may add protection, so do not equate this narrow observation to an observed corrupt schema. `93-127` uses runtime initialization flags for workers/realtime. A ready process can still have an old queue, stale subscription, incorrect audit chain, or delayed payout.

Keep liveness, critical traffic readiness, deployment schema verification, and business-health panels distinct. Add consumer heartbeat/lag, provider freshness, reconciliation variance, support SLA, stale data indicators, upload scanner saturation, incident ownership, and correlation IDs. Optional-provider outages should degrade that workflow rather than unnecessarily kill the entire site. Acceptance: inject failed/stalled consumers, partially applied schema markers, and provider revocation; the right alert fires with actionable references while unrelated safe workflows stay usable. Unknown values must never become zero or green.

### E10 - P1 worldwide operation gap: entity, currency, time, and policy boundaries

**Design requirement requiring cross-module verification.** Country Packs, Regions, pricing, legal acceptances, and financial services already exist. Their existence is not approval to operate every financial/delivery product everywhere. Record legal entity, country/service area, currency, timezone, policy version, tax treatment, and permitted payment rail on authoritative transactions where applicable. Keep restricted OxSteed Credit and restaurant-specific credit distinct.

Finance, legal, operations, and product owners need a per-country launch checklist and evidence approvals. Currency changes must not rewrite historical transactions; time windows need local timezone plus unambiguous stored instants; daylight-saving, all-day, and cross-border scenarios require explicit handling. Acceptance: an unapproved country/product combination is blocked server-side; customer receipts, operational schedules, and finance reports preserve original currency/entity/policy even after profile changes. This report does not certify licenses, insurance, employment classification, or tax registration: qualified owners must determine those requirements.

### E11 - P1 integrity requirement: unified work visibility without a shadow ledger

**Design requirement.** Customer/Business 360, support, work contexts, delivery, and financial controls are already present. Integrate their explicit record references instead of inferring related work from names or copying balances into a new admin ledger. Every status label must identify whether it is proposed, approved, committed, disputed, or externally pending.

Require immutable correlation references, durable domain events, transactionally saved changes/events, idempotent consumers, per-consumer delivery evidence, and safe replay. Payments/stock/support/authorization services remain authoritative. Provider webhooks must tolerate duplicate and unordered delivery; [Stripe's webhook guidance](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks) supports verifying signatures and handling such delivery semantics. Acceptance: replay an approved extra, reversed payment event, stock adjustment, and delivery completion without duplicate money/inventory effects; show the true next action and one timeline across permitted views.

### E12 - P2 design debt with P1 high-risk consequences: operator consistency

**Design requirement with tab-specific evidence below.** Establish one admin design contract: server-bounded data grids, URL-persisted filters, accessible saved views, explicit selection scopes, timezone/currency labels, authoritative refresh, stale-data badges, and per-row error recovery. Financial/destructive bulk actions need preview, affected counts, excluded records, approval limits, progress, resumability, cancellation semantics, and an audit receipt. Never label a retry button as safe until its endpoint is idempotent.

Use task-based workspaces rather than giving 3,500 employees an undifferentiated 65-tab menu. Preserve deep links and role-specific queues. Apply keyboard focus, non-color status cues, clear destructive separation, screen-reader announcements, and Arabic RTL without reversing identifiers. [WCAG 2.2](https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/) is the accessibility reference; this is a target, not a passed conformance audit. Acceptance: representative operators complete their five common tasks without guessing routes or consulting a super-admin, and disabled/unavailable conditions say why and what to do next.

### E13 - P1 privacy/security requirement: controlled investigation and evidence access

**Design requirement.** Users, Conversations, Search Logs, Visitors, audit pages, profiles, and support can collectively reveal highly sensitive histories. Admin login and MFA are already checked (`server/middleware/adminAuth.js:43-71`). Extend that foundation with least-privilege field projections, time-limited sensitive access, reason/case linkage, export approval, secure evidence storage, retention/legal-hold rules, and access-audit review. An audit hash chain is not a substitute for independent storage and separation from database administrators.

Publication-time supply-chain evidence: GitHub's push response on October 2 reported **31 dependency vulnerabilities on the repository's default branch: 18 high and 13 moderate**. The [repository dependency-alert console](https://github.com/oxsteed/old-file/security/dependabot) is the source for triage. This is not a fresh dependency scan of `e7ac9415`, nor proof that every alert is exploitable or present in the deployed artifact. Security/engineering must map alerts to the audited lockfiles and production dependency tree, determine exposure, apply/test safe upgrades or documented mitigations, and record accepted residual risk. A green CI run must not substitute for that triage; applicable unmitigated high-risk findings are expansion blockers.

Use [OWASP ASVS](https://owasp.org/projects/asvs) as a structured application verification reference. Test object authorization, session theft/revocation, CSRF where relevant, unsafe uploads, injection, SSRF, export leakage, and denial-of-service against this implementation. Acceptance: a support agent sees only necessary cases; a financial analyst cannot retrieve private message bodies through related-record APIs; sensitive access is attributable and reviewable; privacy removal preserves legally required financial evidence under the approved retention policy.

### E14 - P1 continuity requirement: provider, configuration, and AI failure handling

**Design requirement.** Stripe, maps/routing, email, SMS, storage, scanner, external calendars, AI, and trackers have separate outages, quotas, permissions, and version lifecycles. Build an inventory with owners, connectivity health, least-privilege setup, rotation/expiry procedures, outbound deadlines, retries, circuit breaking, budget alarms, and explicit unavailable states. External AI output must remain advisory for moderation, pricing, finance, and access unless a narrowly approved deterministic action is separately authorized.

Acceptance: every dependency can be unavailable without losing committed requests or fabricating outcomes; critical secrets/configurations can rotate through a tested procedure; rate limits do not cause unbounded retry storms; spend limits and degraded modes are visible. No report should expose API keys, tokens, passwords, or full configuration backup contents. All providers remain subject to their actual approved country/product capabilities.

### E15 - P1 operating-model requirement: accountable closure, not a collection of tabs

**Design requirement.** Give each domain a business owner, technical owner, trained operator role, on-call escalation, runbook, SLO, and failure backlog. Define handoffs between support, fraud, finance, delivery, security, and engineering. Add immutable approval decisions, queue assignment/claiming, escalation deadlines, business-calendar rules, and follow-the-sun handover. A globally visible queue with no accountable team is not managed work.

Acceptance: for every lifecycle in the final matrix, a failed, abandoned, conflicted, or overdue record lands in an owned actionable queue; its authorized operator can resolve it or escalate without direct SQL or undocumented super-admin intervention. Every tab finding is closed with evidence, explicitly deferred with an exposure limit, or recorded as a release blocker. An unresolved P0/P1 is not renamed a future enhancement to get a green launch.

## Per-tab audit inventory

The following reviews cover all 65 sidebar tabs. The numbered tab headings are the coverage manifest for this document. Supporting detail/sub-workspace routes are included in the relevant tab review and must also be represented in acceptance tests. No tab is certified simply because its route exists. Tab owners must resolve references to shared findings rather than implementing duplicate remedies independently.

# Regular-admin tab reviews

## 1. Dashboard — /admin/dashboard

### Mission / what exists

The dashboard gives leadership and shift operators a platform summary and entry points into existing workflows. `client/src/admin/pages/super/Dashboard.jsx:138` serves both regular and super administrators. Regular admins call `/admin/dashboard`; super admins receive period-based statistics plus activity, geographic summaries, health, and security incidents. Abortable fetches, partial auxiliary loading, incident acknowledgment, and resolution with an entered note already exist. Financial and geographic panels are not evidence of a missing dashboard. The enterprise question is whether those summaries remain correctly scoped, interpretable, and inexpensive when many regional supervisors use them simultaneously.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/super/Dashboard.jsx:149`, `:159`, `:198`, `:211`. API: `server/routes/admin.js:65`, `:161`; statistics: `server/controllers/adminController.js:19`, `server/controllers/superAdminController.js:172`. Authorization: `server/middleware/adminAuth.js:126`, `:169`. Existing regression contracts: `client/scripts/adminRouteContracts.test.mjs:161` and `server/__tests__/adminPermissionScope.test.js:23`.

### Findings

- **T01-F01 — High [design requirement]: regional supervisors cannot obtain a scoped dashboard through the current global grant contract.** The regular endpoint requires `manage_operations`; `requirePermission` deliberately requires an unscoped grant. This is a protective boundary, not a demonstrated regional data leak. Super dashboard endpoints require super-admin status. Delegating a global grant simply to let a country manager see local totals creates excessive authority. Add a distinct scoped summary contract that resolves allowed regions and organizational responsibilities on the server, with an explicit global-view privilege.
- **T01-F02 — High [code observation]: bounded visual output hides potentially expensive aggregate work.** Super statistics execute multiple counts over users, helper profiles, and jobs (`superAdminController.js:190`, `:216`, `:233`); regular statistics use several scalar counts (`adminController.js:24`). A card count is small but its SQL work can grow with the entire population. No load measurement was obtained. Use shared, timestamped aggregates and incremental counters where accuracy permits; verify query plans rather than asserting that every count lacks an index.
- **T01-F03 — Medium [code observation]: failed auxiliary sources can resemble empty business activity.** Settled activity and geo failures become empty arrays (`Dashboard.jsx:181`); failed core statistics can become an empty object (`superAdminController.js:199`). The incident pane has an explicit error, which is a useful existing pattern. Apply that pattern to every source so an unavailable geographic feed cannot be interpreted as zero regional activity.
- **T01-F04 — High [verification gap]: release health lacks complete self-reported provenance.** The supplied healthy `/ready` snapshot has null SHA, digest, and deployment ID. `server/utils/release.js:53` already resolves these fields. Populate and validate this existing mechanism; do not mislabel the deployed image as unknown merely because these fields are null.

### Expansion scenario

At a follow-the-sun handoff, supervisors in Montréal, Paris, and Cairo open the dashboard while a global incident commander compares regions. A delayed aggregate and a failed geo request must not produce an apparent drop in demand. The country manager should see local backlog without receiving customer-management, financial-recovery, or worldwide settings rights. Leadership also needs to distinguish the source timestamp from the time their browser last refreshed, particularly during deployment and recovery.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Show a persistent scope chip, source timestamp, measurement timezone, and per-card available/stale/unavailable state. Resolve scope before aggregation, not after returning global records. Cache equivalent authorized summaries by scope and period with request coalescing. Keep acknowledgment and resolution in the existing incident system; link every exception to its authoritative record. Display observed image provenance alongside readiness, including an explicit incomplete-metadata warning that does not imply liveness failure. Define metric semantics for “active,” “suspended,” and period boundaries with named owners.

### Dependencies / owner

Platform Operations owns metric definitions and incident workflows; IAM owns scoped grants; Data Engineering owns aggregates; SRE owns provenance and capacity evidence. Regional dashboards depend on the existing regions/permission system, not a new role per city.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

With six million synthetic users, realistic job histories, and 200 concurrent authorized dashboard viewers, p95 response time is below two seconds and DB pool wait remains below 100 ms. Repeated refreshes share aggregate work. A region-only operator cannot retrieve global totals by URL or API. Injected geo/query failures display unavailable, never a fabricated zero. Every deployed instance reports the expected SHA, deployment ID, and independently identified image digest; authenticated keyboard and Arabic-layout checks execute without skips.

## 2. Operations Overview — /admin/operations

### Mission / what exists

Operations Overview is an implemented cross-domain command center, not an absent product page. It summarizes delivery, jobs, food orders, support, payments, disputes, queues, integrations, Credit, SLA, risk, privacy/export work, change control, observability, and CRM. Cards point toward existing authoritative modules and carry correlation IDs. Financial, settings, audit, and Credit sections apply additional checks. The client refreshes every 30 seconds only while visible and aborts stale fetches. This is a useful foundation for worldwide incident management; its remaining challenge is delivering actionable, scoped signals without repeatedly aggregating all operational tables.

### Evidence

UI polling and cards: `client/src/admin/pages/OperationsOverview.jsx:177`, `:193`, `:219`, `:262`. Route permission: `server/routes/admin.js:68`. Source summaries and gates: `server/controllers/adminOperationsController.js:15`, `:26`, `:59`, `:114`, `:160`, `:192`. Tests: `server/__tests__/adminOperationsController.test.js:26`, `:61`, `:85`. Shared DB pool default/cap: `server/db.js:17`.

### Findings

- **T02-F01 — High [code observation]: the overview multiplies metadata probes and aggregate queries per viewer.** `statusSummary` and `countTable` first query information_schema, then count records; fifteen sections run concurrently, with nested parallel queries. `LIMIT 50` bounds returned statuses, not rows examined by `GROUP BY`. At 200 visible viewers, 30-second polling alone averages approximately 6.7 overview requests per second, before manual refreshes. This arithmetic is a scenario, not an observed outage. Cache schema capability information and coalesce shared scope summaries with explicit freshness budgets.
- **T02-F02 — High [design requirement]: command-center access is global and its nonfinancial sections lack regional variants.** The route requires global `manage_operations`. Additional gates correctly deny regional financial grants; the tests verify that safeguard. Delivery, support, risk, and CRM still need server-side scope-aware aggregates for local managers. Separate region-scoped observation from platform-wide intervention, with auditable emergency access rather than routine super-admin promotion.
- **T02-F03 — Medium [code observation]: status totals do not convey incident ownership or queue urgency.** The support card groups statuses; the SLA card counts breaches; observability counts failed events/webhooks (`adminOperationsController.js:125`, `:160`, `:192`). These are real signals, but they lack oldest-item age, current owner, impact region, and remediation deadline in this response. Add those projections from existing incident, SLA, delivery-recovery, and routing records, without copying their mutable state into a second command-center database.
- **T02-F04 — Medium [verification gap]: provider connection counts do not prove delivery recovery.** Connected integrations and pending webhook counts (`:144`) describe stored state, not a provider round trip or reconciliation result. The healthy readiness snapshot proves dependencies were ready at 08:55 UTC, not that every optional integration or country-specific provider was usable.

### Expansion scenario

A European webhook backlog develops while North American support volumes spike and Credit reconciliation is healthy. A shift lead must identify the oldest failed delivery, its tenant, and the accountable team without opening fifteen modules or seeing unrelated customer data. An incident commander should compare global impact and delegate local work. If 200 operators refresh simultaneously during this incident, observability reads must leave database and worker headroom for the actual recovery operations.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Keep existing section isolation, correlation IDs, and hidden-tab polling behavior. Add scope selection constrained by server grants, source-generated timestamps, maximum-age indicators, and clickable oldest exceptions. Poll with jitter and shared caching; favor event-triggered invalidation for acute incidents. Expose provider capability states separately from connected-record counts and distinguish unsupported, unconfigured, degraded, and healthy. Place owner, acknowledgment, due time, and last recovery result beside exceptional counts. Extend the existing incident workflow for assignment and escalation; retain domain pages as the write authority.

### Dependencies / owner

SRE owns query budgets and polling; domain teams own exception definitions; Support Operations owns SLA projections; IAM owns region/tenant scope; Integration Operations owns provider evidence. Read models must preserve Credit visibility restrictions already implemented.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

With 200 overview viewers, one summary refresh performs bounded shared work per scope, p95 remains below two seconds, and unrelated transactional p95 degrades by less than 10%. Injected source failure affects only its card and retains a traceable correlation ID. Country operators cannot query other regions or restricted finance sections. Every open exception exposes owner and deadline or explicitly identifies itself as unassigned. Demonstrate one webhook/provider outage and successful replay in an isolated environment; publish the resulting evidence independently of a green `/ready` response.

## 3. Users — /admin/users

### Mission / what exists

Users is a substantial administration interface with search, role/plan/status/location filters, sorting, page sizes, CSV export, profile/detail links, messaging entry points, and privileged account actions. The quick-create modal uses the full account-creation endpoint. Password recovery, deletion scheduling/restoration, impersonation, verification, notes, and richer detail views already exist elsewhere in the same control plane. The standard list excludes admin/system identities. Enterprise readiness requires making this existing lifecycle scalable and delegable while reducing routine exposure of six million customers' identifying and financial information.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/UsersList.jsx:19`, `:46`, `:99`, `:185`; URL/filter contracts: `client/scripts/adminRouteContracts.test.mjs:22`. List SQL: `server/controllers/superAdminController.js:536`, `:585`, `:616`, `:632`. Routes: `server/routes/admin.js:137`, `:181`, `:196`; regional support: `server/middleware/regionAuth.js:119`, `:163`. Export: `superAdminController.js:2427`. Existing safe deletion documentation in code begins at `:2475`.

### Findings

- **T03-F01 — High [design requirement]: a global customer-management grant is too broad for routine country support.** List/detail/name/ban endpoints use global `manage_users`. Region matching already supports country boundaries (`regionAuth.js:66`), but those standard endpoints do not use record-scoped authorization. Do not claim regional grants bypass the middleware: they are denied. Add narrowly scoped read, contact, status, verification, and recovery rights with explicit tenant/region predicates and field masks. Retain super-only role changes and impersonation as privileged workflows.
- **T03-F02 — High [code observation]: list refreshes include population-wide summaries and per-user business calculations.** Rows include correlated job counts and payout sums (`superAdminController.js:608`); summary counts ignore current filters (`:632`). Search uses several substring predicates, and offset sorting lacks a unique tie-breaker (`:553`, `:621`). The bounded 100-row cap is valuable but does not establish acceptable cost at deep pages or six million users. Separate heavy financial/customer detail from routine identity lookup and benchmark representative queries before choosing indexes.
- **T03-F03 — Medium [code observation]: exports are synchronous, capped, and disconnected from list filters.** `exportData` selects the newest 10,000 rows (`:2436`) and builds the CSV in memory. It already uses no-store headers, formula escaping, auditing, and a sensitive-operation limiter; those controls should remain. An enterprise investigator needing a complete filtered cohort needs a durable export job and manifest, not an apparently complete partial file.
- **T03-F04 — Medium [code observation]: custom account modals lack full dialog semantics.** The create/reset overlays use plain container elements and backdrop clicks (`UsersList.jsx:120`, `:203`). Close buttons have labels, but these inspected structures do not themselves provide a dialog role, focus trap, or focus return. This is a concrete accessibility implementation gap; end-to-end assistive-technology behavior remains unverified.

### Expansion scenario

A regional fraud team investigates 80,000 accounts after a credential-abuse campaign while ordinary agents recover customer access. The investigator needs a reproducible filtered export, but a helpdesk agent should not gain financial totals, global bans, or impersonation. Two operators working the same user need a conflict-aware status screen and authoritative history. A keyboard-only supervisor must be able to open, complete, cancel, and leave every recovery modal without losing focus or editing the wrong identity.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Show scope and redacted identity fields until a purpose-based reveal is authorized and logged. Use cursor pagination with `(sort_value, id)` tie-breakers and separate scoped aggregate summaries. Add filter-preserving asynchronous exports with requester ownership, row counts, checksum, expiry, cancellation, and audit reference. Expand existing deletion and recovery guards rather than adding another account store. Prefer invite/reset links and approved recovery workflows over routine staff-entered passwords; policy and jurisdiction decisions remain with Security and Legal. Use shared accessible dialogs with identity confirmation and conflict refresh.

### Dependencies / owner

Identity Engineering and Security own customer lifecycle and privileged recovery; IAM owns workforce delegation; Data Engineering owns query/export capacity; Accessibility and localization owners validate the UI. Financial fields require the existing finance permission model.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

Six-million-user lookup and cursor navigation meet p95 below 1.5 seconds with 250 concurrent agents. Regional negative-access tests cover lists, details, counts, exports, and mutations. An 80,000-row export has complete filter metadata and resumes after a worker restart without duplication. Stale status changes return an actionable conflict. All recovery dialogs pass keyboard focus/return and screen-reader checks across five supported languages; no credentials appear in audit logs or artifacts.

## 4. Jobs — /admin/jobs

### Mission / what exists

Jobs provides an implemented searchable, status-filtered list, detail links, pagination, export, and separately granted hard deletion. Abortable loading retains earlier rows after a failed refresh. Server lists use bounded pagination and denormalized category names. Job actions include cancel/remove/flag/unflag; deletion requires a reason and allows only selected statuses, with a status predicate that detects concurrent changes. These existing safeguards deserve credit. The readiness concern is safe intervention across millions of historical jobs, especially where cancellation intersects accepted work, escrow, disputes, and audit durability.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/JobsList.jsx:34`, `:49`, `:67`, `:80`, `:218`; detail route: `client/src/admin/AdminApp.jsx:232`. API grants: `server/routes/admin.js:75`. List/action/deletion: `server/controllers/superAdminController.js:1029`, `:1045`, `:1088`. Payment relationship: `server/migrations/014_phase4_create_payments.sql:7`. Existing state/modal regression: `client/scripts/adminJobsList.test.mjs:11`; pagination helper: `server/utils/pagination.js:8`.

### Findings

- **T04-F01 — High [code observation]: administrative cancellation directly changes job status without a financial recovery workflow in this handler.** `forceJobAction` updates any noncancelled job to cancelled (`superAdminController.js:1056`), rather than limiting the source state or calling a lifecycle/escrow coordinator. The reason is optional on the API. Other payment/recovery systems exist; this observation does not prove all funds are lost or all consumers ignore the change. Require a dependency-aware action plan that distinguishes unpublished content, accepted work, in-progress service, completed history, and disputes before committing cancellation.
- **T04-F02 — High [code observation]: hard deletion and audit are not one durable operation.** The status-guarded DELETE is followed by best-effort audit with swallowed failures (`:1102`, `:1106`). The original payment FK cascades from jobs (`014_phase4_create_payments.sql:7`); later schema/runtime behavior must be verified before asserting actual production cascade loss. Replace routine deletion with archival and evidence preservation; gate irreversible purge on dependency checks, policy approval, and a durable audit transaction.
- **T04-F03 — High [confirmed defect]: first-time flagging can incorrectly return a conflict.** The flag UPDATE predicate is `NOT (COALESCE(metadata,'{}')->>'admin_flagged') = 'true'` (`:1067`). If the key is absent, extraction yields SQL NULL, the predicate is unknown, and no row matches. A legitimate first flag is therefore reported as already flagged/state changed. Use a null-safe comparison and a targeted absent/false/true-key regression. This is source-level SQL reasoning, not a live reproduction.
- **T04-F04 — Medium [code observation]: paging and export do not support stable large investigations.** List sorting uses only created_at with OFFSET (`:1039`); UI export ignores current filters (`JobsList.jsx:70`), and the shared export caps rows at 10,000. Introduce stable cursors and filtered asynchronous exports, preserving existing grant, limiter, and no-store controls.

### Expansion scenario

A hurricane response team needs to suspend marketplace exposure for 20,000 affected jobs while preserving already accepted service obligations and payment records. Moderators in several countries must not cancel completed paid work simply because a content report arrives. Two supervisors can act on the same job during a handoff; one should receive a meaningful state conflict. An investigator must reproduce the exact affected cohort, including archived records, instead of exporting a worldwide newest-jobs sample.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Present “hide listing,” “cancel service,” and “archive record” as distinct, authorized actions with an impact preview. Require reasons server-side, retain before/after versions, and coordinate escrow/refund requests through existing idempotent financial services. Show pending/recovering/failed compensation states and receipt links; do not mark a refund complete from a job status alone. Use durable batch jobs with preview counts, per-item outcomes, checkpoints, and bounded retries for incident cohorts. Currency and provider eligibility must be reviewed by the responsible finance/provider teams for each jurisdiction.

### Dependencies / owner

Marketplace Operations owns job semantics; Payments owns escrow and compensation; Trust and Safety owns content hiding; Data Governance owns retention/purge policy; IAM and SRE own scope and throughput.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

Absent/false/true flag states produce correct outcomes. Concurrent cancellation and acceptance cannot commit an invalid lifecycle transition. Canceling a paid/completed job requires an explicit allowed policy and recovery receipt. Every destructive action has durable audit evidence even if auditing fails. A 20,000-job isolated batch resumes after restart and reports every item exactly once. Six-million-user-derived job histories support p95 list reads below two seconds; regional API and export denials execute in the authenticated matrix.

## 5. Reports — /admin/reports

### Mission / what exists

Reports is the content-report intake and review queue. It already shows pending/reviewed/escalated/dismissed status tabs, reporter details, descriptions, job/user deep links, and dismiss/warn/remove/escalate actions. Errors render explicitly rather than claiming the queue is clear. The backend uses bounded pagination and locks each report during review, returning 409 for a previously processed report. The richer reports dashboard is linked from the list. Those features form a real review workflow; the enterprise gaps are queue reachability, faithful decision states, ownership, and consistent downstream effects across different target types.

### Evidence

UI loading/actions/statuses: `client/src/admin/pages/ReportsList.jsx:11`, `:28`, `:40`, `:149`, `:177`. API: `server/routes/admin.js:71`; list/review: `server/controllers/adminController.js:50`, `:89`, `:105`, `:121`, `:135`, `:207`. Localized report contracts: `client/scripts/adminReports.test.mjs:11`, `:18`. The support-report replay test at `server/__tests__/adminMessageIdempotency.test.js:225` covers a different workflow and does not prove content-report decision idempotency; that property needs its own behavioral acceptance.

### Findings

- **T05-F01 — High [confirmed defect]: the UI cannot navigate the server's paginated report queue.** The request sends only status (`ReportsList.jsx:29`); the page renders the returned array with no pagination controls. The server defaults to 25 and returns total/page/limit (`adminController.js:56`, `:76`). With 500 pending reports, operators can only access the initial 25 through this list until decisions remove them. Reviewed or escalated historical pages remain inaccessible from the status tab. Add cursor navigation, queue position, and a distinct oldest-unowned view.
- **T05-F02 — Medium [confirmed defect]: dismiss decisions do not enter the displayed dismissed state.** The UI requests `status=dismissed`, but review sets every non-escalate decision to `reviewed` (`adminController.js:129`). A newly dismissed report therefore cannot appear in the dismissed tab through this handler. Resolve the contract with an explicit decision outcome and separate lifecycle status, or align the UI taxonomy; migrate/report old outcomes carefully rather than inventing historical decisions from localized strings.
- **T05-F03 — High [confirmed defect]: remove-content can report success without handling the target.** The action is offered for any pending report, but removal handles only jobs and reviews (`adminController.js:135`). Other target types can be marked reviewed without a corresponding removal or an unsupported-target response. For jobs, removal directly cancels status without the dependency-aware financial workflow described in T04-F01. Resolve supported targets explicitly and delegate to existing content/lifecycle services.
- **T05-F04 — High [code observation]: the durable decision and audit/notification effects are separated.** The report row is transactionally locked, which prevents duplicate decisions; however audit failures after COMMIT are swallowed (`:207`), and warning notification failure is only represented in the response (`:218`). The UI discards that response and shows generic success (`ReportsList.jsx:47`). Preserve existing conflict protection while making notification and audit delivery visible and recoverable.

### Expansion scenario

A coordinated spam event produces 50,000 reports across multiple languages. Duplicate reports concern the same job, while one genuine high-risk complaint is older but has fewer submissions. Regional reviewers need ownership, language, due time, and linked evidence; supervisors need to inspect reviewed history even when thousands of cases remain pending. A warning provider failure must appear as an unresolved delivery task rather than a successful warning. A review decision should be understandable independently of the language used by the original operator.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Extend the existing reports and reports-dashboard records with assignment, severity, locale, service region, case grouping, and SLA projections. Preserve `FOR UPDATE` and add version-aware claim/reassignment. Store machine-readable outcome and policy revision separately from localized display labels. Render supported-target actions only after authorization and capability checks; enforce identical checks on the API. Add a durable outbox for decision notifications and transactional audit, plus explicit delivery pending/failed states. Link evidence snapshots and appeals without creating another reporting database. Bulk triage should run as a previewed, scoped asynchronous job with individual decisions and audit references.

### Dependencies / owner

Trust and Safety owns case taxonomy and policy; Marketplace/Payments own job effects; Messaging owns notification recovery; IAM owns regional reviewer scope; Support Operations coordinates escalated customer cases.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

An operator can reach all 500 fixture reports and their reviewed history. Dismiss produces the agreed dismissed outcome. Unsupported targets return 422 or the agreed explicit error without changing the report. Two simultaneous reviews produce one decision and one meaningful conflict. Provider/audit failure never appears as complete delivery. A 50,000-report batch resumes without duplicate warnings; five-language keyboard workflows and regional negative-access checks execute without skips.

## 6. Moderation — /admin/moderation

### Mission / what exists

Moderation is a distinct implemented job review queue, derived from admin flags and pending content reports. It shows job descriptions, client information, categories, report counts, and detail links, with dismiss/unflag, flag, and cancel actions. Fetches are abortable and failed reads show a retry panel. It complements Reports, which records individual complaints, and Content Removals, which handles bids/reviews. Enterprise work should connect these existing views into consistent case decisions, not replace them with a new moderation platform or falsely label the page missing.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/Moderation.jsx:17`, `:25`, `:81`, `:108`. Queue SQL: `server/controllers/adminController.js:237`, `:253`, `:267`. Permissions: `server/routes/admin.js:73`, `:78`. Job mutation semantics: `server/controllers/superAdminController.js:1045`, `:1065`, `:1074`, `:1083`. Related report locking is present at `adminController.js:105`.

### Findings

- **T06-F01 — High [code observation]: the moderation queue has no row bound or server pagination.** Its SELECT joins jobs, users, categories, and reports, aggregates report counts, sorts, and returns every matching job (`adminController.js:242`). The UI renders the entire array (`Moderation.jsx:81`). At large abuse-event volumes this can create expensive queries, large payloads, and long browser render work. This is inspected unbounded behavior, not a measured performance failure. Page the authoritative query before rendering and benchmark reporting indexes with real cardinalities.
- **T06-F02 — High [confirmed defect]: “Dismiss” does not resolve report-driven queue membership.** The button invokes `unflag` (`Moderation.jsx:110`), whereas the queue includes any job with pending reports (`adminController.js:260`). A job with pending reports remains in the queue after its admin flag is cleared; a report-only job can return 409 because there was no flag (`superAdminController.js:1076`). Expose “clear admin flag” accurately and provide a linked report-review action with explicit case outcomes.
- **T06-F03 — High [code observation]: read and write permissions differ without a task-specific UI gate.** Queue read requires global `moderate_content`; actions require global `manage_operations` (`admin.js:73`, `:78`). The component offers all buttons without checking the latter grant. Server enforcement correctly blocks unauthorized writes, but a moderator can enter an action/reason only to discover that authority is unavailable. Add task-specific rights and matching UI availability, retaining server enforcement as authoritative.
- **T06-F04 — Medium [design requirement]: raw report counts do not establish severity or ownership.** Sorting by count then job creation age (`adminController.js:268`) favors repeated complaints without representing verified severity, evidence freshness, assigned reviewer, or review deadline. Add policy-based triage and owner claims; do not assume high report volume alone establishes harmful conduct. The first-flag NULL predicate defect is tracked once under T04-F03 rather than counted as another independent bug here.

### Expansion scenario

Thousands of reports target legitimate listings during a coordinated campaign while a single dangerous listing receives one report. A bilingual Caribbean team and an Arabic-speaking team review separate territories. The queue must prioritize credible urgency, explain why a job appears, and avoid two reviewers canceling the same active service. Clearing an administrative flag should not make the reviewer think the underlying complaints were dismissed. A language specialist should inspect evidence without inheriting worldwide cancellation or financial intervention rights.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Show queue-entry reasons separately: admin flag, pending report group, automated signal, and reopened appeal. Link each reason to the existing Reports record and show assignment, language, impact region, and due time. Use stable cursors, virtualized bounded lists, and scoped saved filters. Replace prompt-driven interactions with accessible decision dialogs displaying current state, allowed actions, and financial dependencies. Require reason/policy metadata on the API; support conflict refresh and unflag semantics distinct from dismissing complaints. For large incidents, use an asynchronous batch with preview, approval thresholds, individual results, and cancellation of unfinished work.

### Dependencies / owner

Trust and Safety owns triage and adjudication; Marketplace owns job effects; IAM owns scoped read/write roles; Payments owns dependent recovery; Accessibility owns dialogs. Existing content reports remain the case source of truth.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

A 100,000-job flagged fixture returns at most 100 queue rows per request and meets p95 below two seconds at 150 reviewers. Report-only, flag-only, and combined cases expose correct actions and remain in or leave the queue for explicit reasons. A read-only moderator sees no usable mutation controls and receives 403 on direct API writes. Concurrent reviewers cannot commit contradictory decisions. Urgent single-report cases can outrank low-risk duplicates, with a documented policy explanation and complete localized audit history.

## 7. Enforcement — /admin/enforcement

### Mission / what exists

Enforcement provides an implemented action list with filtering, pagination, issue/deactivation workflows, and confirmation for lifting actions. The backend has a warning/restriction/suspension ladder, writes restrictions and user suspension state transactionally, and records enforcement audit events. Deactivation correctly targets restriction rows by enforcement ID and checks for other active suspensions before lifting the user lock. Appeals already have a schema and indexes. The audit therefore evaluates consistency and delegation of these existing mechanisms, rather than asserting that enforcement or appeals do not exist.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/AdminEnforcementDashboard.jsx:14`, `:27`, `:89`, `:380`. Routes: `server/routes/enforcement.js:14`. Enforcement writes: `server/controllers/enforcementController.js:24`, `:48`, `:93`, `:150`, `:173`; deactivation: `:359`, `:388`, `:398`. Existing appeals: `server/migrations/173_enforcement_appeals.sql:4`, `:27`. Global permission guard: `server/middleware/adminAuth.js:126`.

### Findings

- **T07-F01 — High [confirmed defect]: suspension issuance erases underlying active restrictions.** Issuing a suspension calls `liftUserRestrictions`, which deactivates all active restriction rows for the user (`enforcementController.js:63`, `:150`). Their enforcement-action records are not deactivated by that helper. Later lifting a suspension checks other suspensions but does not restore still-applicable restrictions (`:396`). A bidding restriction intended to outlast a short suspension can therefore stop applying afterward. Preserve independent sanctions and compute effective permissions from all current actions instead of destroying subordinate effects.
- **T07-F02 — High [code observation]: overlapping issue/deactivate operations lack a common user-level lock.** Both handlers use transactions, but issuance does not serialize the user's sanction set, and deactivation reads the action before BEGIN (`:365`) then checks other suspensions (`:398`). Transactions alone do not prove correct concurrent composition. Add a user-scoped lock, atomic version checks, and effective-state recomputation; test an issue racing with deactivation and expiration. This finding does not claim a reproduced production race.
- **T07-F03 — Medium [code observation]: invalid restriction types broaden to “all.”** `applyUserRestriction` converts an unknown type to the broadest restriction (`:30`). The endpoint validates actionType but delegates restriction normalization to this helper. A typo from an API client should return a validation error, not suspend unrelated capabilities. Require explicit restriction enums, valid future expiry where applicable, and validated target/report relationships before committing a sanction.
- **T07-F04 — High [design requirement]: worldwide delegation and appeals need bounded adjudicator rights.** Current enforcement requires global `manage_users`; audit read uses a separate grant. Extend the existing appeals/action schema with region/case assignment, reviewer independence, due times, and an escalation route. Existing appeals tables are a dependency, not a missing capability assertion. Eligibility, notices, and retention policy require jurisdiction review; no legal rule is inferred here.

### Expansion scenario

A user has a 30-day bidding restriction and receives a two-day suspension from another regional team. At suspension expiry, the original bidding restriction must still apply. During an appeal, one supervisor lifts an incorrect action while another issues a new restriction. The result must be explainable from durable actions and policy versions, without a last-writer-wins user boolean accidentally reopening access. A supervisor should see the necessary history and appeal evidence only within assigned responsibility.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Represent actions as independent records with explicit active/effective/expired/lifted states. Retain the existing transactional audit and enforcement ID links. Recompute effective capability denial under a shared user lock and publish revocation/realtime consequences through the existing authentication system. Require an explicit lifting reason, display remaining actions and resulting access before confirmation, and separate issuance from appeal approval when policy demands it. Add narrow warning/restriction/suspension/lift permissions with regional targets checked on the server. Show expiry timezone, ownership, case links, and whether notification was delivered.

### Dependencies / owner

Trust and Safety owns sanction composition; Security owns authentication/session enforcement; IAM owns adjudicator scope; Support owns notices and appeals handoffs. Legal and market operations review policy content independently of implementation.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

The 30-day restriction survives the two-day suspension and its lift. Invalid types return 400 without writes. Fifty concurrent issue/lift/expiry attempts preserve the exact effective action set and audit sequence. Expired actions do not incorrectly block deactivation or access. Regional users cannot inspect or sanction foreign cases. Appeal queues expose owner/deadline and retain evidence links; keyboard and localized issue/deactivate dialogs pass executed browser checks.

## 8. Content Removals — /admin/content-removals

### Mission / what exists

Content Removals is an existing administrative workspace for bids and reviews. It has debounced search, status filters, page navigation, reason collection, hide/remove actions, review restoration, retryable fetch errors, and visible restore failures. The server requires `moderate_content`, validates reasons for removal, conditionally updates the current state, and commits the content change and audit row in one transaction. This is stronger than a purely cosmetic control. Enterprise expansion must preserve those safeguards while distinguishing a harmless visibility correction from a change to accepted marketplace obligations or reputational calculations.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/ContentRemovals.jsx:13`, `:104`, `:113`, `:136`, `:148`, `:167`. Routes: `server/routes/admin.js:83`. Backend list/actions: `server/controllers/adminController.js:389`, `:447`, `:509`, `:571`. Conditional update and audit: `:466`, `:475`, `:528`, `:537`, `:588`, `:596`. Initial job/payment dependency: `server/migrations/014_phase4_create_payments.sql:7`.

### Findings

- **T08-F01 — High [code observation]: bid removal accepts any status other than already removed.** The handler fetches bid/job information, checks only `status === 'removed'`, then changes any remaining bid to removed (`adminController.js:454`, `:466`). No accepted-bid/job dependency check appears in this handler. Other lifecycle services may exist, so this is not proof that an active job fails in production. Require an explicit policy for accepted/awarded bids, preserve commercial evidence, and route service cancellation or financial effects through existing domain services rather than a status-only removal.
- **T08-F02 — Medium [code observation]: concurrency is detected but reported as a generic server failure.** Conditional updates prevent two operators hiding/removing/restoring the same state, which is valuable. Zero updated rows throw ordinary errors (`:470`, `:532`, `:592`) and outer handlers return 500. An expected conflict during a 3,500-employee handoff should be 409 with current state, actor, and safe refresh guidance. Preserve atomic auditing and distinguish conflicts from actual database failures.
- **T08-F03 — High [verification gap]: visibility, rating aggregates, search, and customer notices need consistency evidence.** Review hide/restore handlers change `is_public` and audit it (`:528`, `:588`); the inspected handlers do not themselves recalculate ratings or issue an owner notice. That does not prove no trigger/consumer handles these effects. Trace and test the full read path, caches, profile ratings, and any notice obligations before claiming completion. Extend the existing review ecosystem with an explicit recovery task where necessary.
- **T08-F04 — Medium [code observation]: restoration lacks decision context and the removal modal lacks full dialog semantics.** Restore sends no reason (`ContentRemovals.jsx:150`), and its audit contains rating/reviewer metadata but no restoration justification (`adminController.js:600`). The custom modal begins with plain divs (`ContentRemovals.jsx:39`). Add explicit policy/reason and accessible focus handling; end-to-end assistive behavior has not been established by source contracts alone.

### Expansion scenario

A regional team hides 8,000 fraudulent reviews, another reviews appeals, and an account manager attempts to remove a bid already accepted by a customer. The team must preserve the original records, reverse mistaken hides, and explain rating changes. Two reviewers restoring the same item should get a clear conflict rather than a retry loop that creates confusing audit attempts. No country team should see or act on unrelated tenant content solely because it holds a global moderation grant.

### Exact recommended controls and UX

Add a scoped content preview with linked job, accepted-work state, author, policy, evidence snapshot, and visibility history. Separate hiding public content from altering commercial obligations. Keep reason-required removal and transactional audit; extend the same standard to restoration. Make expected-state/version required and return typed conflicts. Use existing notification/recalculation infrastructure with durable delivery receipts and repair queues, not a new ratings database. Provide previewed asynchronous bulk hides/restores with per-item validation and failure reporting. Mask contact details unless needed for the reviewer task and authorize every selected item server-side.

### Dependencies / owner

Trust and Safety owns removal policy and appeals; Marketplace owns bid obligations; Reputation Engineering owns rating/search/cache consistency; IAM owns region/tenant scope; Accessibility owns dialog behavior. Legal reviews retention and notification policy by jurisdiction.

### Measurable acceptance criteria

Accepted/awarded bid fixtures cannot be removed without the approved dependency-aware action. Concurrent hide/restore attempts yield one accepted transition and typed conflicts, with atomic audit. An 8,000-review isolated bulk job resumes after interruption and identifies every outcome. Public content, search, and rating projections converge within the agreed 60-second target or expose repair status. Restorations require reasons and preserve original evidence; keyboard focus and regional read/write denials pass executed tests.

## 9. Skills — /admin/skills

### Mission / what exists

Skills is an implemented lookup-taxonomy manager, not a missing capability. It supports category filtering, debounced search, paginated rows, add/edit, active/hidden toggles, category autocomplete, and permanent deletion requests. It links to the existing licenses manager. The API bounds list size and offset; permanent deletion is explicitly super-admin-only even though other authorized admins can manage lookup entries. The expansion problem is governing a shared taxonomy used by global profiles and matching, including translations, aliases, referenced IDs, review ownership, and safe concurrent edits.

### Evidence

UI: `client/src/admin/pages/SkillsManager.jsx:16`, `:25`, `:38`, `:50`, `:89`, `:111`, `:121`, `:142`. API: `server/routes/admin.js:649`, `:688`, `:715`, `:736`. Initial schema/indexes: `server/migrations/037_skills_and_licenses_lookup.sql:5`, `:11`. Related job skill storage: `server/migrations/080_job_required_skills.sql:5`, `:9`; business references: `server/migrations/079_business_skills.sql:16`.

### Findings

- **T09-F01 — Medium [confirmed defect]: clearing an existing category does not clear the stored value.** UI edits submit an empty category; the API converts it to null and applies `COALESCE($2, category)` (`admin.js:720`, `:725`). The old category survives even though the form can appear cleared. Distinguish omitted fields from an explicit empty/null update and return the actual persisted state. Apply the same partial-update convention consistently to taxonomy metadata.
- **T09-F02 — High [code observation]: creation can silently overwrite an existing skill and edits have no version precondition.** POST uses `ON CONFLICT(name) DO UPDATE` (`admin.js:695`), so an employee adding an apparently new entry can change another curator's category or active status. PUT overwrites fields by ID without checking a version (`:719`). These paths also lack an audit write in the inspected handlers. Add conflict-aware create semantics, stable IDs, versioned updates, and transactional taxonomy history; do not report mere duplicate-name validation as sufficient governance.
- **T09-F03 — High [design requirement]: the shared schema is a single-name taxonomy rather than a worldwide concept model.** `skills_lookup` contains one unique name and category; its search index is English full text, while admin search uses ILIKE (`037_skills_and_licenses_lookup.sql:7`, `admin.js:657`). This is not proof every skill search is unindexed or that licenses are missing. Extend existing IDs with translated labels, aliases, locale-aware lookup, deprecation/merge mapping, and regional applicability instead of creating one disconnected catalog per language.
